We say such a copy of H is a rainbow copy of H and if t = |E(F)|, we say it is a transversal copy of H. As an example, one of the most famous generalizations of the Brualdi-Stein
- **Conjecture 1.2** (p. 13): 4 ((Aharoni & Berger, 2009)). Given a collection of matchings M1, . . . , Mn on the same vertex set V , where |Mi| ≥n + 1 for each 1 ≤i ≤n, we can find a rainbow matching M of size n. Note that if we identify M1, . . . , Mn as a properly colored multigraph on V , then the AharoniBerger conjecture is equivalent to the existence of a rainbow matching in a properly colored multigraph where the number of colors is n and each color induces a matching of size n + 1. Thus, the Ryser conjecture is a special case of the Aharoni-Berger conjecture. The AharoniBerger conjecture has been verified asymptotically in (Pokrovskiy, 2018); however, the full conjecture remains open. There have been several other types of problems involving the search for rainbow matchings in properly colored multigraphs. In these problems, it is often required that the graphs in the collection are sparse, meaning that either each graph is a single matching or its maximum degree is bounded. But what if the graphs in the collection are dense? For example, if the minimum degree of each graph is at least some linear fraction of |V |, and we aim to find more complex structures.

Currently, the only known tight bounds for these type of problems are Hamiltonian cycles in simple graphs and δk−1,M in kuniform hypergraphs where M is a perfect matching(see (Lu, Wang, & Yu, 2021)). No other tight bounds are known. In (Gupta et al., 2023), the authors asked about characterizing the stability of all such kinds of transversal embedding problems. As mentioned in previous Chapters, a transversal bandwidth theorem (Theorem 4.1.1) was proved in (Chakraborti, Im, et al., 2023), which gives an asymptotically tight minimum degree bound (χ(H) −1)/χ(H) guaranteeing the existence of a transversal copy of H for many graphs H. On the other hand, there are many graphs where this threshold is not tight. For example, when H is a 2-factor – a spanning 2-regular graph – (El-Zahar, 1984) conjectured that the minimum degree threshold should equal (n + r)/2 where r is the number of odd cycles. Note that if H consists only of even cycles, then (χ(H) −1)/χ(H) = 1 2, while otherwise it equals 2 3.

Let G = (G1, . . . , Gn) be a graph collection on a common vertex set of size n. Suppose that δ(G) ≥(n + r)/2. Does G contain a transversal copy of H? Abbasi’s proof uses the regularity-blow-up method. Later, a proof avoiding this method was found by Khan (Khan, 2011), although the broad structure of the proof is similar. An even harder problem is a transversal version of the Bollobás-Eldridge-Catlin Conjecture (see (Bollobás & Eldridge, 1978), (Catlin, 1976)).

## Yelena Yuditsky (2018)

*Properties of typical T-free graphs* · [thesis](https://mcgill.scholaris.ca/server/api/core/bitstreams/f039b7ba-08fb-402a-b18c-6ebbdf58cd6e/content) · [record](https://mcgill.scholaris.ca/handle/20.500.14905/96705)

- **Conjecture 1.0.1 (Erd ̋os-Hajnal [25])** (p. 12): Let H be a graph, then there is an " = "(H) > 0, such that every H-free graph contains a stable set or a clique of size at least |V (G)|"(H). The Erd ̋os-Hajnal conjecture is still open and appears to be very difficult to resolve (see a survey in [17]). In some cases it is useful to partition a given graph G into a minimal number of stable sets. For example, in the famous four colour problem, we want to colour countries on a map with only 4 colours, such that any two neighbouring countries receive di↵erent colours. For this problem it is possible to define a graph whose vertex set are the di↵erent countries, and two vertices which represent two countries have an edge between them if and only if they are neighbouring on the map. A stable set in this case represent a set of countries such that no two countries share a border. The number of colours needed to colour the map is equal to the minimal number of stable sets in which we can partition the graph. The chromatic number of a graph G, which is denoted as χ(G), is the minimal number of stable sets into which it is possible to partition the vertex set V (G) of G.

Then there is an integral affine manifold with singularities B and maps φ : X →B, ˇφ : ˇX →B which are dual special Lagrangian torus fibrations which means that a non-singular point in B corresponds to a special Lagrangian torus in X and a dual torus in ˇX. In the conjecture, finding the base B is very technical part and inspired Gross and Siebert to study toric degenerations of Calabi-Yau manifolds as described in [GS0]. The idea is to deform to a degenerate central fiber and construct the base B using tropical geometry which gives rise to a mirror algebra that can be thought of as a ring of functions on its mirror space. In [69] it was shown that Shift can be computed
- *stated as open* (p. 384): 3 Open Problems We end this chapter by listing a few open problems.
- **Question 108** (p. 385): What is the wire complexity of the Shift operator in the unrestricted model? For the Shift operator, the upper bound is ̃O(n1+1/d) while the best known lower bound is Ω(n · λd(n)) for constant depth d ≥3. Currently, it is not clear which one is closer to the truth.
- **Question 1** (p. 387): Is it possible to give good general lower bounds on the AC0 complexity of Subgraph(P) in terms of the treewidth of P only? Prior to our work, Rossman [73] answered this question in affirmative for the case of a k-clique by proving a lower bound of Ω(nk/4) on the AC0 complexity of Subgraph(Kk). Generalizing Rossman’s method, Amano [9] gave a general lower bound that holds for arbitrary patterns P. It in particular implied an nΩ(k) lower bound (and, thus, an affirmative answer to Question 1) for the k × k grid Gk,k: this result is very interesting since Gk,k is the “canonical” example of a sparse graph with large treewidth. Before discussing our results, it will be convenient to introduce the following handy notation: given a pattern P, we let C(P) be the minimal real number c ≥0 for which Subgraph(P) is solvable on n-vertex graphs by AC0 circuits of size nc+o(1). In this notation, the previous results mentioned above can be stated as C(P) ≤tw(P) + 1 ([8, 9], P any pattern), C(Kk) ≥k 4 [73] and C(Gk,k) ≥Ω(k) [9]. Our contributions. We explicitly formulate and study two modifications that already played a great role in the previous research.
- *stated as open* (p. 388): What happens to C(P) at the opposite side of the spectrum, say, for bipartite patterns P, remains wide open.
- *stated as open* (p. 390): The chapter is concluded with a brief discussion and a list of open problems in Section 4.
- *stated as open* (p. 442): While it remains an open problem whether C(P) is (even approximately) minormonotone under general AC0 reductions, this result strongly suggests that the colorful version of the subgraph isomorphism problem is much better structured and well-behaved than the standard (uncolored) one.
- **Question 165** (p. 447): Is it possible to give general lower bounds on the worst-case AC0 complexity of Subgraph(P) (uncolored P-subgraph isomorphism) in terms of the treewidth of P only? When P is a core, we know that C(P) = Ccol(P) = eΘ(tw(P)). At the opposite end of the spectrum, Question 1 is wide open for bipartite patterns P. The next two questions seek to improve the parameters in our main results.
- **Question 166** (p. 447): Can the upper bound Cave(P) ≤2κ(P) + O(1) of Theorem 110 be improved to κ(P) + O(1)?
- **Question 167** (p. 447): Can the log tw(P) factor be eliminated from our lower bounds on κcol(P) (Theorem 109) or at least Ccol(P)?
- *stated as open* (p. 447): 6 Conclusion and Open Problems With the results of this chapter, the state of knowledge on the average/worst-case AC0 complexity of the uncolored/colorful P-subgraph isomorphism problem now stands as Ω( tw(P) log tw(P)) ≤κcol(P) ≤Ccol(P) ≤tw(P) + 1 ≤ C(P) ≤ κ(P) ≤Cave(P) ≤2κ(P) + O(1).
- **Question 168** (p. 448): Is C(P) minor-monotone or at least monotone under subgraphs? More modestly, if Q is a minor (or subgraph) of P, is there a reduction from Subgraph(Q) to Subgraph(P) by AC0-circuits of size O(nc) for a constant c independent of P and Q? That would imply C(Q) ≤O(C(P)); currently we do not know if C(Q) can be bounded by any function in C(P). Finally, it would be interesting to investigate the relationship between κcol(P) and the complexity of Subgraphcol(P) beyond AC0. In particular, we recall the result of Marx [57] that Subgraphcol(P) has no no(tw(P)/ log tw(P))-time algorithm unless the Exponential Time Hypothesis (ETH) fails. Follow-up work of Alon and Marx [5] looked at the question of removing the log tw(P) factor loss in the exponent of this result (toward the goal of showing that nΘ(tw(P)) is the true complexity of Subgraphcol(P), at least assuming the ETH). Alon and Marx specifically identified constant-degree expanders as a case where “substantially different methods” are needed to eliminate the log tw(P) factor loss incurred by the reduction of [57].
- **Question 169** (p. 448): Can it be shown that Subgraphcol(P) has no no(κcol(P))-time algorithm unless the ETH fails?
